### Those flowing down their influences upon all lovers

## of true <term>Hermetic Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Hermetischen Weißheit." Refers to the esoteric tradition of alchemy and philosophy attributed to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus.</note>

# Kind Reader,
## of whatever rank and station you may be.

If you have a desire for the art of <term>Chemistry</term> <note>The original uses *Chymie*, a term that in the 17th and 18th centuries referred to the inseparable combination of practical chemical science and the spiritual/speculative pursuits of alchemy.</note> and are willing to understand and learn something within it without suffering harm, then this <term>Treatise</term> <note>original: *Tractat*</note> can serve you and be useful. This is because the author is not envious of his knowledge, but rather will consider himself fortunate if he should learn that he has established something fruitful through this work. As the saying goes:

> Blessed is he whom the dangers of others make cautious,
<note>original Latin: *Felix, quem faciunt aliena pericula cautum*</note>
happy is the man who becomes wise through the misfortunes of others!

Yet the author does not wish this to be understood as if he, through his own harm, has made other people clever and

<sig>)( 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: wise</meta>

<summary>This page serves as the opening of the author's preface, inviting readers of all social standings to study the art of chemistry. The author expresses a selfless desire to share knowledge so that others may learn from previous mistakes without suffering their own financial or physical losses.</summary>

<keywords>preface, chemistry, alchemy, instruction, caution, wisdom, author's intent</keywords>

# Preface.

<margin>
Con
N:
</margin>

wise; by no means. Instead, because **trial** <note>original Latin: *periculum*. In the 17th century, this word carried a double meaning: it could mean "danger," but in a scientific context, it meant an "experiment" or "attempt."</note> also signifies an attempt (during which one might often expect less danger), the author wishes that through his trials and experience—which brought him no harm but rather benefit—others may also learn something and become wise.

He who first breaks the path in dangerous places has, without question, earned more glory than those who follow him on that path, even if the followers often improve it and make it more convenient. The same holds true for the sciences and arts. The primary glory remains with their inventors; for if they had not been there to lay a foundation, no one would have been able to build upon it. For **one cannot desire what one does not know** <note>original Latin: *ignoti nulla cupido*</note>. Had there been no **Hermes** <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary "Thrice-Great" figure to whom the origins of alchemy, magic, and various sciences were attributed in antiquity.</note>, posterity would have known nothing of **Hermetic Philosophy** <note>The philosophical and alchemical tradition based on the purported writings of Hermes Trismegistus, focusing on the relationship between the divine, the cosmos, and the material world.</note>. After this great Egyptian **Philosopher** had laid the foundation for this 

<meta>catchword: secret- (or "goes")</meta>

<summary>This page justifies the author’s work by explaining that his "trials" are intended as helpful experiments for the reader rather than warnings of failure. It further argues that original inventors, particularly Hermes Trismegistus, deserve the highest honors for providing the foundations upon which all later knowledge is built.</summary>

<keywords>preface, experimental trial, scientific discovery, Hermes Trismegistus, Hermetic Philosophy, history of science, inventors</keywords>

secret art, many have ventured to build upon it, though with unequal skill; and indeed, most have only contributed straw and stubble, whose work, however, burns easily, and quite often to the great harm of other people.

Some, however, have erected gold and nobler things upon it, and their work is constant and remains firm. <note>This is a biblical allusion to 1 Corinthians 3:12-15, suggesting that the "fire" of experience or alchemical testing will destroy worthless theories (straw) but prove the value of true knowledge (gold).</note>

Here, the author has undertaken to place a philosophical heaven upon the <term>Smaragdine Foundation</term> <note>original: *Smaragdinischer Grund*. This refers to the *Tabula Smaragdina* or "Emerald Tablet," a foundational Hermetic text said to contain the secrets of the universe.</note>, which **Hermes** laid; yet not like **Paracelsus** <note>The 16th-century physician and alchemist known for his complex and often intentionally obscure writings.</note>, whose <term>Heaven of the Philosophers</term> <note>original Latin: *Cœlum Philosophorum*.</note> is a <term>Book of Vexations</term> <note>original Latin: *Liber vexationum*. In the alchemical tradition, a "vexation" refers to a text designed to frustrate the reader or test their patience and resolve through riddles.</note>, since the aforementioned **Paracelsus** brings these both—the **Heaven of the Philosophers** or the **Book of Vexations**—under a single title, over one single treatise. No! The author does not wish to **vex** <note>To frustrate or confuse the reader.</note> the lovers of this difficult art (which is yet easy), or to leave them gaping expectantly and nothing

<summary>The author distinguishes his work from those who have built "flimsy" alchemical theories, promising instead a solid "philosophical heaven" based on the teachings of Hermes. He specifically critiques Paracelsus for writing in a way that intentionally frustrates or "vexes" students of the art.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Emerald Tablet, Paracelsus, Heaven of the Philosophers, Book of Vexations, alchemical tradition, Hermeticism</keywords>

put into it, as **Paracelsus** did; rather, he wishes to fix upon this heaven the two great lights <note>The Sun and the Moon, which in alchemy represent Gold and Silver.</note> along with the other planets and certain stars. Their motion, orbit, conjunction, appearance, reflection, eclipses, and the like shall show that those who wish to have a share in this transitory heavenly kingdom <note>A metaphor for the alchemist's laboratory work or the physical world of matter.</note> will also find a certain satisfaction therein.

The lights of the great heaven create day and night, and provide signs and seasons, years and days. Exactly the same will be observed in the <term>Philosophical Heaven</term> <note>original Latin: *Coelum Philosophorum*. This refers to the internal environment of the alchemical vessel where the "planets" (metals) interact.</note>, in which **the Sun** <note>Gold</note>, **Mars** <note>Iron</note>, **Venus** <note>Copper</note>, **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>, **Mercury** <note>Quicksilver</note>, **the Crucible**, etc., create and provide the day—that is, the **golden tinctures**; while **the Moon** <note>Silver</note>, **Jupiter**, **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, **Tartar**, **Mercury**, **Antimony**, **the Sun**, etc., create and provide the night—that is, the **silver tinctures**. <note>In alchemy, a "tincture" is a potent substance capable of transmuting base metals or healing the body. Golden tinctures were considered of a higher "solar" grade than silver "lunar" ones.</note>

The path indeed to this heaven is covered with countless thorns, and

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: if</meta>

falls, however, often by chance and unexpectedly, that someone either hears of this Art or happens to see a book that treats it; when he then reads therein how this Art far surpasses many others—in that it promises, alongside the fullness of wealth, also the most excellent means of health for a long life, and other splendors more (among which the pleasure of the mind is not the least)—he immediately conceives a burning desire to know and learn this Art. One asks for books that deal with it, and also endeavors to become acquainted with people who know of such an Art, or are at least regarded as such. But now it happens very rarely that one encounters an <term>Adept</term> <note>original: *Adeptum*. In the alchemical tradition, an Adept is someone who has successfully mastered the "Great Work" and possesses the secret of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> who reveals himself; even those who only know how to work with the uncommon and not yet universally known <term>separation of metals</term> <note>original: *Scheidung der Metallen*. In this context, an amateur or enthusiast who is passionately devoted to the study of alchemy but has not yet achieved success.</note> becomes acquainted with one who is also still searching, yet has learned something and can make a great boast of it, then he is even more taken by the love of the <term>Art</term> <note>original: *Kunst*. A standard term for alchemy, often called "the Royal Art."</note> and persuades himself of great things. Whatever he reads in an alchemical book—especially if it is quite clear and easy to understand—or whatever a hypocritical <term>decoy-bird</term> <note>original: *Schein-heiliger Lock-Vogel*. A metaphorical term for a charlatan or "sophist" who lures others into false alchemical pursuits with sweet, deceptive promises.</note> whistles to him with a sweet voice, he hears with wonder and believes so firmly that he allows no one to talk him out of it. Now that he is so completely overwhelmed by his <term>desire</term> <note>original: *Begierde*. A menial laborer hired to tend the laboratory furnaces. Alchemists often sought uneducated assistants to ensure their secret processes would not be understood or stolen.</note> must be hired, so that such a person can grasp nothing and cannot "chatter out of school" <note>An idiom meaning to reveal secrets or gossip about private affairs.</note>.

Some are so cautious that they undertake completely unnecessary things for the sake of appearances, only to keep their art even more secret; as if the true and innocent <term>Alchemy</term> <note>original: *Alchymie*. The author is likely referring to "Chymia," the chemical art of transmuting metals or preparing medicines.</note> were a piece of knavery, and its <term>lovers</term> <note>original: *Liebhaber*. one does not yet rightly know the 
<term>hand-grips</term> <note>original: *Handgriffe*. This refers to the specific manual skills, knacks, or practical maneuvers required in a laboratory setting that cannot be learned from books alone.</note>.

from the start, and tries it now

when nothing succeeds for him at all, 
he becomes discouraged and 
begins to doubt the Art. 
But by then it is already too late. 
For his small means are gone, 
no foundation for a significant understanding 
is present, and where he 
should have begun—namely, with doubting—
there he stops, despairing of himself 
and the entire Art. Thus, 
most alchemists wander toward the

[most] they must ascend, like the 
alchemists, yet they do not 
stand still when they reach the 
eleventh step, that is, when they 
begin to doubt, but only then 
do they begin to become truly 
wise. They doubt first the 
possibility of the matter, thereafter 
the doctrine of this matter and the 
certainty of its teachers; 
furthermore, the ability of their 
own understanding; yet they do 
not reject the matter, but read 
anew and judge; they try 
anew, and pay heed to 
their work and its <term>accidents</term> <note>original: *Zufälle*. In early modern science and alchemy, "accidents" refers to the observable qualities, symptoms, or unexpected phenomena that occur during a chemical reaction.</note>; 
they also test themselves, 
whether they alone are equal 
to the task; and accordingly 
seek opportunity to watch others, 
how they perform this or 
that labor; in such a manner 
they receive from day to day 
more light and enlightenment, 
until, according to the divine 
will, through labor, <term>reason</term> <note>original: *Vernunfft*. The intellectual capacity to discern natural laws.</note>, 
and <term>patience</term> <note>original: *Gedult*. A central alchemical virtue, as the "Great Work" was said to require long periods of time and steady temperament.</note>, 
they enter into the midst of 
the <term>philosophical heaven</term> <note>A metaphor for the successful completion of the alchemical process and the attainment of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> 
and possess its glory.

<summary>The author explains that true alchemical adepts distinguish themselves by using doubt as a tool for deeper study and observation rather than a reason to quit. Through persistent labor, reason, and patience, they eventually achieve the "philosophical heaven" of successful transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>alchemists, doubt, eleven steps, philosophical heaven, reason, patience, methodology, enlightenment</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page follows the introduction of the virtues required for alchemy. The catchword "Ar-" from the previous page identifies the first word of this page, "Arbeit" (Labor).</meta>

## Preface.

Labor, reason, and patience are three powerful companions on this journey; whoever lacks these may fish in their bed or in the air until the Day of Judgment, yet they will not find what they seek. One must marvel that nowadays some people—otherwise quite clever in their own professions—have begun to philosophize about this art, whom I will not name out of respect. Their <term>matter of the stone</term> <note>original Latin: *Materia Lapidis*. The raw starting material required to produce the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is said to be a heavenly and earthly being, having no name of its own, found everywhere, yet recognized by no one; it supposedly requires no effort or labor, but rather makes itself. One must have pity on such people. Whoever has read the writings of the true <term>philosophers</term> <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists who sought the hidden truths of nature.</note> and is himself reasonable will very easily grasp that nothing can be accomplished in this art without effort and labor—specifically in the <term>first preparation of the bodies</term> <note>original: *ersten Zubereitung der Cörper*. In alchemical practice, "bodies" refers to solid metals or minerals. The "first preparation" is the difficult initial stage of cleaning, grinding, or dissolving these materials.</note>. For this art does not deal with soft eggs, but with hard stones and metals; to soften these and [bring them] into a high

<summary>The author emphasizes that alchemy requires hard work, reason, and patience, mocking those who believe the "Philosopher's Stone" can be created without effort or from a magical, self-forming substance. He asserts that the initial processing of hard metals and minerals is a physically demanding task that cannot be avoided.</summary>

In the great firmamental heavens, the Sun holds the preeminence among all stars. In the mineral kingdom, however—which the originators of <term>Alchemy</term> <note>The ancient art and science of transformation, seeking to perfect both materials and the human spirit through chemical and philosophical labor.</note> compare to the heavens—Gold stands in the center, and surpasses all **Metals** and **Minerals** in brilliance and magnificence.

### The Origins of Gold.

Regarding its beginnings, many have written many things, especially the modern writers, among whom **Basilius Valentinus** <note>A celebrated (though likely legendary) Benedictine monk and alchemist; his writings are central to the transition between medieval and modern chemistry.</note> made a beginning with his **three principles** <note>original Latin: *tribus principiis*</note>, namely **Salt**, **Sulfur**, and **Mercury** <note>original Latin: *Sale*, *Sulphure* and *Mercurio*. These are the *Tria Prima*, the three fundamental substances that alchemists believed composed all material things.</note>; he was followed thereafter by **Theophrastus Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note> and others, without the slightest rational cause. Those who are somewhat older held **Sulfur** and **Mercury** as the **constitutive principles of metals** <note>original Latin: *pro principiis constitutivis metallorum*</note>; and while these have indeed stepped somewhat closer to the truth, they have not reached it completely. The most ancient ones rested their case with **Mercury** alone—

<summary>This page introduces Gold as the "Sun" of the mineral kingdom and begins a historical overview of how different eras of alchemists defined the fundamental principles of metals.</summary>
<keywords>Gold, Sun, Alchemy, Basilius Valentinus, Paracelsus, Three Principles, Salt, Sulfur, Mercury, Tria Prima, mineral kingdom</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of alchemical theories from the previous page, specifically concluding the thought that earlier writers attributed the differences in metals to the ripening of Mercury and "mixed earth." It transitions from theoretical origins to a practical definition of Gold as "ripened Quicksilver."</meta>

highly and solely content, and have attributed the distinction between metals to the ripening of **Mercury** <note>original Latin: *Mercurii*</note>, alongside a certain mixed earth. This opinion, insofar as it is applied only to the **proximate matter** <note>original Latin: *ad materiam proximam*; the immediate substance from which a thing is formed, as opposed to the ultimate or "remote" primal matter.</note>, is the most correct; for one can demonstrate and prove this through experience in the decomposition of metals and metallic minerals, no matter how much certain "learned ignoramuses" <note>A dismissive term for scholars who have theoretical knowledge but lack practical, hands-on experience in the laboratory.</note> may cry out against it. 

The very first and remote matter, however—which in itself is nothing but a heap of joined and intermixed dust particles—is best known to Nature. For this reason, nothing further shall be discussed here, as this writing will not encompass **theory** <note>original Latin: *theoriam*</note>, but only **practice** <note>original Latin: *praxin*</note>, to which I turn without further delay.

## What is Gold?

Gold is a very pure metallic body, entirely simple, and in its essence a perfectly ripened **Quicksilver** <note>Mercury</note>, without any other addition. This can be maintained through Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy—the practical work of the laboratory.</note> without effort against the learned fools and detractors. For just as the entire substance of common, purified Quicksilver, through mere **digestion** <note>A process of gentle, sustained heat used to "ripen" or mature a substance over time, mimicking the natural heat of the earth.</note> into the

...[fi]nest gold is matured; From which one sees and must conclude that **quicksilver** <note>mercury</note> is an **unripe gold**; Likewise, ripe common gold can be entirely reduced and turned back into running quicksilver; from which it also follows that gold must be a **ripe quicksilver**. For this true reason, the silly imaginings of the **Tri-principists** <note>Adherents of the "Tria Prima" theory, which held that all metals were composed of three principles: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury. This was a cornerstone of Paracelsian alchemy.</note> fall flat. For everything that **Basilius** <note>Basilius Valentinus, a semi-legendary 15th-century alchemist and monk whose works were widely published in the 17th century.</note> and his flock call the **Salt and Sulfur of the Sun** <note>original Latin: *Sal und Sulphur Solis*; in alchemical shorthand, the "Sun" (Sol) always refers to Gold.</note> is, when viewed in the light of day, nothing other than a refined gold which, through further labor, becomes quicksilver.

## The Mercury-making of Gold.
<note>original Latin: *Mercurificatio Solis*.</note>

The conversion of Gold into Mercury is, however, easiest and most natural when it is performed **by means of Mercury** <note>original Latin: *per Mercurium*</note>, that is, common quicksilver. For this reason, even the most ancient philosophers commanded: **Make Mercury through Mercury** <note>original Latin: *Fac Mercurium per Mercurium*.</note>; for through this, the entire substance of the gold is transformed into pure quicksilver without any loss. 

Such a task, however, requires understanding and its specific **steps** <note>original Latin: *gradus*</note>, the most important of which are (1) **Calcination** <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures, often to reduce it to a powder or "ash."</note>, (2) **Amalgamation** <note>The process of combining gold with mercury to form a soft paste or alloy.</note>, (3)

<summary>The author argues that gold is simply "ripened" mercury and describes the "Mercurificatio Solis," the process of reducing gold back into its liquid mercurial state. He rejects the Salt-Sulfur-Mercury theory of Basilius Valentinus, favoring the idea that mercury is the sole fundamental substance of gold.</summary>

**Digestion** <note>original Latin: *Digestio*; a process of gentle, prolonged heating, similar to incubation.</note>, (4) **Distillation** <note>original Latin: *Destillatio*</note>. Through these four **operational stages** <note>original Latin: *gradus operationis*</note> the gold is **reduced** and turned into quicksilver. From this, afterward, through mere boiling, can be made not only a **medicine** for human bodies, but also a **tincture** for **metals**.

### Calcination of Gold.
<note>original Latin: *Calcinatio Auri*. Calcination is the process of heating a metal until it oxidizes and turns into a fine powder or "calx."</note>

**G**old, however, is **calcined** in various ways, yet among them all, the following way is the best:

First, dissolve common salt in pure boiled or distilled water; **separate** the **dregs** <note>original Latin: *feces*</note>, **distill** the water from it, let the salt **crystallize** <note>original German: *schießen*, literally "to shoot," referring to the rapid formation of salt crystals</note>, and dissolve it again until it contains no more impurities but dissolves completely pure. Keep this salt protected from filth.

Then, next, take fine gold that has been cast through **antimony** <note>original Latin: *Antimonium*; a method of purifying gold by melting it with antimony to remove base metals.</note> or **reduced from the quartering** <note>original: *aus der Quart reduciret*; "Inquartation" was a process where gold was alloyed with three parts silver so that nitric acid could dissolve the silver, leaving the gold pure.</note>. **Flatten** it into thin sheets <note>original: *laminire*</note> and **amalgamate** <note>combine into a soft paste with mercury</note> it with as much **purified mercury** <note>original: *☿ii purgati*</note> as is necessary. For example: **Take** <note>original symbol: ℞</note> 1 **Loth** <note>A historical German unit of weight, roughly 14–15 grams or half an ounce.</note> of gold that has been beaten thin; add 4 Loth of **purified mercury**. Amalgamate it with special diligence, and do not let the rubbing vex you <note>The physical grinding of mercury and gold was labor-intensive.</note>. When it is well-amalgamated, press the **amalgam** through a smooth leather so that the excess mercu-

Wash the remaining **amalgam** <note>original: *Amalgama*; a mixture of mercury and another metal, in this case, gold.</note> quite clean with salt and vinegar, and finally with pure water, until it has become brighter than a mirror; dry it, and it will become hard so that it allows itself to be pulverized. **Take** <note>indicated by the symbol ℞, the standard abbreviation for the Latin *Recipe*</note> this **amalgam**, and grind it with three times its weight of the purified salt; rub it with the greatest diligence in a warmed glass mortar, and a black powder will form from the amalgam and salt. Put this into a suitable glass retort <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids or volatile solids</note>, attach a receiver with pure water, and distill all the **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*</note> over with the appropriate fire. Whatever attaches itself to the neck of the retort, bring out carefully with a clean feather or a small brush, so that it does not fall back in.

Upon that which remained in the retort, pour pure water so that the salt therein may dissolve; boil the salt out completely, and you will obtain a delicate **gold-calx** <note>original: *Gold-Kalck*; a fine metallic powder or "ash" produced through chemical processing, often the first step in creating a medicinal tincture.</note>. Amalgamate this again with this same **mercury** <note>indicated by the chemical symbol ☿</note> and treat the amalgam with washing and cleaning as at first; afterward, distill it, and repeat this several times, and thus the gold is well calcined. However, this is only a coarse **calcination** <note>original: *Calcinatio*</note>; nevertheless, it is necessary, for it greatly accelerates and promotes the subsequent work.

## 2. Amalgamation.

Now amalgamate this delicate powder with two parts of purified **mercury** <note>original symbol: v̄; the text specifies *mercurii purificati*</note> in a glass mortar. Wash the **amalgam** <note>original: *Amalgama*; a mixture of mercury and gold</note> quite clean, lay it upon a clean glass dish, cover it with a clean paper so that no dust falls upon it, and let it become dry. Afterward, form small pellets from it, as large as lentils, with cleanly washed hands; lay these again on the glass dish in a warm place so that the moisture of the **elemental water** <note>the residual cleaning water</note> evaporates. Then, have ready a clear and bright vial of such size and proportion as the adjacent figure shows, and also of such strength; put your **amalgam** <note>represented by the symbol āāā</note> into it, which should weigh 3 **Loth** <note>a traditional unit of weight, roughly 15 grams or half an ounce</note> and no more. The upper part of the vial's neck must be ground, and the stopper must also be properly ground-in with **emery** <note>a coarse abrasive powder used to create airtight glass-on-glass seals</note>, so accurately that no water can run out. When the **amalgam** <note>original symbol: āāā</note> is placed into such a small vial, set it in the **Athanor** <note>a specialized alchemical furnace designed to maintain a steady, gentle heat over long periods</note> in warm sand. At first, close the vial only with paper until all the wateriness has **evaporated** <note>original: *evaporirt*</note>, which happens in a day and night with gentle heat. Afterward, when one

### 3. *Digestion* <note>original: *Digestio*; a process of slow, constant heating used to mature or decompose a substance.</note>

One must maintain it at such a *degree of fire* <note>original: *gradu ignis*; the specific intensity of heat applied to the vessel.</note> until it falls apart into a dark powder. In the meantime, however, strange things will appear in the glass—yet never twice the same, nor the same in one glass as in another, even if one has the exact same starting mixture and the same heat to a hair's breadth. This I can certainly affirm from much experience. Here, I wish only to report the most principal occurrences and changes in the *matter* <note>original: *Materie*</note> within the glass that I have observed. When the *amalgam* <note>The original uses the symbol *aaa*, the standard alchemical shorthand for an amalgam.</note> has stood for approximately 15 to 16 days in proper *digestion*, it grows upward like little trees <note>This refers to "arborization," the formation of tree-like crystals, which alchemists saw as a sign of "life" within the metals.</note>; on the walls of the bulb of the vial, *mercury* <note>original: *Mercurius*, represented by the symbol ☿.</note> attaches itself and coats the glass like a mirror. ...brought to completion. This calx <note>A powder produced by heating or oxidizing a metal; in this context, the refined state of gold.</note> is now properly <note>original: *propriè*</note> the **philosophical gold** <note>original: *Aurum philosophicum*</note> and the **proximate matter of the Philosopher's Stone** <note>original: *materia proxima Lapidis Philosophici*; the immediate substance from which the final goal can be made</note>, from which one can begin the work both in a **particular** <note>original: *particulariter*; referring to the transmutation of specific metals for immediate profit</note> as well as a **universal** <note>original: *universaliter*; referring to the creation of the Great Elixir that heals all things</note> manner.

Since beginners in this art are often less concerned with the underlying truth of the matter than they are with making an honest profit from it—and in such a way that they might have it fairly soon—I will first report how one should put this **gold-calx** to use in a **particular** way. This is so that one can better attend to the High Work <note>The "Great Work" or *Magnum Opus*—the long, spiritual process of creating the ultimate Philosopher's Stone</note> while still being able to earn one's bread. One should have 3 **Loth** <note>A traditional German unit of weight, roughly equivalent to half an ounce or 15 grams</note> of this **philosophical gold** <note>original: *Auri Philosophici*</note> (however, if one initially started the work with double the amount—which is wise and well-done, because one can then use one half for the **particular** and the other for the **universal**—it is even better).

**Recipe:** <note>original: ℞, the traditional symbol for a medical or alchemical recipe</note> Take 3 **Loth** of this **fixed philosophical gold** <note>original: *Auri Philosophici fixi*</note>, place it in a vial, and set it in a **sand bath** <note>original: *Sand-Capelle*; a container filled with sand and heated from below to provide a steady, even temperature</note>. Once the sand is quite hot, let the glass and the material become thoroughly heated. Afterward, pour **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*</note> that has been warmed through a warmed glass funnel onto the **philosophical gold** <note>original: *Aurum Philosophicum*</note>. (Note: take 1 **Loth** of the **prepared mercury** <note>original: *☿ii præparati*</note>—

**loth** of **philosophical gold** <note>original: *Auri Philosophici*</note>. Now divide these 8 **loth** into 3 equal parts; put each part into a vial, and **imbibe** <note>original: *imbibire*; to soak or saturate the dry powder with liquid mercury</note> each part with 1 1/2 **loth** of **prepared mercury** <note>original: *☿ii præparati*</note>. **Digest** <note>original: *Digerirs*; to heat a substance slowly in a sealed vessel, often for weeks</note> it for 4 weeks **by degrees** <note>original: *per gradus*; gradually increasing the temperature</note>, and **fix** it <note>original: *figirs*; to render a substance stable so it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to fire</note>. From this, take 3 **loth**; melt some silver, and project the **precipitate** <note>original: *Præcipitat*; the solid alchemical powder</note> into it. **Part** <note>original: *scheide*; to separate the gold from the silver, usually through a chemical bath</note> the silver, and you will thus obtain three **loth** of gold.

The remainder you should **imbibe** again with 3 **loth** of **prepared mercury**; in this way, you can **reduce** <note>original: *reduciren*; here meaning to transform the alchemical substance into physical metal</note> 3 **loth** every month. Even if you value the **loth** at only ten **thalers** <note>Large silver coins used across Europe</note>, it still amounts to 360 **thalers** throughout the entire year. A contented person can already live quite peacefully on this, even after accounting for 60 **thalers** in expenses.

However, he who wishes to spend more—either because his rank and office require it, or because he wishes to work for the benefit of the poor—can increase his **philosophical gold** until he has 30 **loth** of it. This does not even require a great deal of time. He can then **reduce** 10 **loth** monthly, which makes 1200 **thalers** annually, calculating the **loth** at only ten **thalers**. One could multiply this work even further, according to how much one might need, and yet all of this could be performed in a single furnace with the same effort and labor.

<summary>This page provides a financial and practical plan for an alchemist to maintain a steady income by transmuting silver into gold using a "philosophical gold" catalyst. It outlines how a practitioner can scale their production to live modestly or support charitable works using a single furnace.</summary>

Indeed, this **fixed precipitate** <note>original: *Præcipitatus fixus*; a solid substance that has been rendered stable so it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to fire</note> is not only a good household gold mine, but also an excellent medicine, which will yield nothing to **Van Helmont’s** <note>Johannes Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a famous Flemish chemist and physician</note> **Horizontal Gold** <note>original: *Auro Horizontali*; a celebrated medicinal gold preparation described in Van Helmont's works</note>; of which more shall be mentioned in the **Chapter on Venus** <note>original: *Cap. de Venere*; in alchemy, Venus is the traditional symbol for copper</note>.

This would thus be a work—albeit a tedious one—that is easy to accomplish through the **particular way** <note>original: *Via particulari*; a chemical process intended to transform a specific amount of base metal into precious metal, rather than creating a universal catalyst</note>.

If one now wishes to make from this the **Universal** <note>original: *Universale*; the "Universal Medicine" or Philosopher’s Stone, capable of transforming any amount of matter</note>, or a **Tincture**, which transforms all mercurial bodies into true gold, then one must **radically** <note>original: *radicaliter*; to the very root or fundamental level</note> unlock this **Philosophical Gold** <note>original: *Aurum Philosophicum*</note>, and increase and heighten it in both color and fluidity, which is the actual **sublimation** <note>original: *Sublimatio*; in alchemy, this refers to the purification and spiritualization of a substance, elevating it to a higher state</note> of the **philosophers**; which I will place here under the title

## 4. **Distillation** <note>original: *Destillatio*</note>

For in this operation, the gold actually becomes mercury—so much so that one can distill it over completely, just like common mercury. However, this is not necessary; rather, it distills by itself in its vessel after the **radical dissolution** <note>original: *radicalen Dissolution*; a total chemical breakdown of a solid into liquid form</note> has occurred. Some, who have been a little more sincere and clear, such as **Hermes** in his **Emerald Table** <note>original: *Hermes in seiner Tabula [Smaragdina]*; the foundational cryptic text of Western alchemy attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</note>...

## The Process of the Red Tincture. <note>original: *Processus Tincturæ rubeæ*</note>

Take the **white sulfur**, while it is still warm, and **imbibe** <note>original: *imbibire*; to saturate a solid with a liquid</note> it several times with **Mercury**, until the whole becomes a soft and somewhat dark **mass**; keep it in **digestion** <note>original: *Digestion*; the application of gentle, consistent heat to encourage chemical maturation</note> for 30 days, so that the dark color will pass away; digest it for another 30 days, and it will turn green, afterwards yellow, and various colors, and finally become a high-red powder. This red powder is called the **red sulfur of the Sages**. **Ferment** this, or melt it with fine **gold**, just as was done before with the **silver** and **white sulfur**; **imbibe** it also just the same as with the **white work**, and so, in its own time, the **red tincture** will be finished.

If you now wish to perform **Projection** <note>original: *Projection*; the final act of casting the alchemical medicine onto base metals to transform them into gold</note>, you may take 3, 4, or 5 parts of **gold**, let it flow <note>meaning to melt it into a liquid state</note>, and place 1 part of the **Tincture** upon it, and melt them well together; thus a brittle, glass-like mass will come of it, though not transparent. With this, one performs **Projection** upon **Mercury** and other **imperfect metals** <note>original: *unvollkommene Metalla*; metals like lead, tin, or copper which the alchemist believed could be "ripened" into gold</note>. The power of the Tincture, or

## Multiplication of the Tincture <note>original: *Augmentatio Tincturæ*</note>

The multiplication of the **Stone** is nothing other than a repetition of the **Work**: Namely, one takes 1 part of the **Tincture**, whether it be white or red, and 2 parts of the **Ferment**, melts them together, amalgams them with **Mercury**, and dissolves them in **gentle digestion** <note>original: *Digestione leni*; the application of low, steady heat, similar to an incubator</note>. Thus, in a short time, the work is completed, and the **Tincture** is increased both in its power and its quantity. One may read of this in the works of the authors, primarily the **Anonymous Philaletha** <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a highly influential but pseudonymous 17th-century alchemist</note>, as well as **Ripley** <note>George Ripley, a famous 15th-century English alchemist</note> and **Jean d’Espagnet**, who have written of this most sincerely and clearly, though with some differences between them.

## On the Prepared Mercury <note>original: *De Mercurio præparato*</note>

For the work of the **Philosophical Tincture**, or the **Philosophers' Stone** <note>original: *Lapidis Philosophici*</note>—which is nothing other than a subtilized and sublimated **Gold**—there must necessarily be an **Agent** <note>original: *Agens*; the active force or catalyst in the chemical process</note> or active being that can bring about such subtilization and elevation; since indeed all

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessary components for the Great Work, specifically identifying the active agent required to transform gold into the Tincture. It follows the previous page's introduction to the "Prepared Mercury."</meta>

philosophers primarily require. Such an **Agent** <note>original: *Agens*; the active force or catalyst in a chemical reaction</note> in this **process** <note>original: *Processu*</note> is **Mercury**, or common running, yet **prepared Quicksilver**, which will seem strange to many, especially to those who cannot be satisfied with the simplicity of nature. But one leaves the whims to the dreamers <note>original: *Grillenfängern*; literally "cricket-catchers," a 17th-century term for people lost in useless or melancholy fancies</note> and follows nature, property, and possibility.

Indeed, the **Mercury**, as it is used for the alchemical work, is not simply the common sort, or a mere running shop-quicksilver <note>original: *Kram-Queck-Silber*; commercially available mercury sold by merchants</note>, but is, in truth, the **Philosophical Mercury**; indeed, even a "son of the philosophers." For it is prepared through rational art by an intelligent **Philosopher**, in the same way that **Philosophical Gold** <note>original: *Aurum philosophicum*</note>, also called the **Sun of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Sol Philosophorum*</note>, is named for no other reason than that it was made by a philosopher (albeit from natural **subjects** <note>original: *Subjectis*; the raw materials or base substances used in the experiment</note>). This, the supernatural visionaries—to whom everything must be called "philosophical" that is merely foolish, irrational, and against all nature—will hardly admit.

This philosophical **Mercury** now, as it is required for the true work of the Alchemists, is prepared from common quicksilver without the addition of any foreign substance in the following manner.

First, make a **Regulus of Antimony with Iron** <term>Regulus Antimonii ♂; a "regulus" is the metallic heart of a mineral, and the symbol ♂ indicates the use of iron (Mars) in the process</term>. Take iron plate, or a thinly drawn iron rod, and let it become red-hot. In the furnace, melt as much **Antimony** as you desire, then thrust the glowing iron or steel rod into it; the **antimony** will dissolve as much of the iron as the **Sulfur of Antimony** <term>Sulphur Antimonii; the volatile, reactive component of the mineral</term> can overcome. The purer part of the **antimony** <term>Spieß-Glas; a historical name for stibnite or crude antimony ore</term>, along with the volatile solar essence of the iron, separates from the coarse and burning sulfurs. The purer part falls to the bottom and is called the **Regulus** <note>literally "little king," so named because this refined metal settles at the bottom of the crucible</note>, while the coarser part remains on top as slag. Knock off the **Regulus**; however, save the slag and use it for the purpose which will be treated in the **Chapter on Mars** <note>original: *Cap. de ♂te*; the symbol ♂ represents Mars, the alchemical association for iron</note>. Of the **Regulus**, take ½ lb. and no more at once, crush it slightly, and let it melt in a clean crucible. As soon as it melts, add finely ground and warmed saltpeter to it, so that it [be:]

One must, however, rub it with particular diligence—the longer and more thoroughly it is rubbed, the better. For this purpose, one can have a machine built, which does not cost much, as such a device can also be used and utilized for other things. In such a machine, the work will not be so burdensome; the labor is easier, and the result is more significant than if one were required to perform this laborious task with bare hands. Yet, those who have no opportunity for such a device must work by hand like a **Hercules**; for this reason, some have also called this work of preparing the **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurii*</note> a **Herculean labor**.

[it must] be washed so long until it no longer emits any blackness <note>original: *Schwärtze*; this refers to the oxidation or impurities that rise to the surface during the grinding of mercury and metal</note>, and becomes as clear as a mirror; then, one puts the **amalgam** <note>original: *Amalgama*; a soft paste made by mixing mercury with another metal</note> into a small glass retort, places it in a sturdy bowl in a sand bath over a charcoal fire, attaches a receiver containing water, and distills all the **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*</note> over into it; the silver then remains behind at the bottom. In such a manner, the **mercury** would be distilled once; this **distillation** <note>original: *Destillatio*</note> is also called **sublimation** <note>original: *Sublimatio*; the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then cooling it back into a solid or liquid</note>, because the volatile golden essence of the iron, which is in the **regulus** <term>the pure metallic heart of a mineral, in this case likely a "star regulus" made from antimony and iron</term>, improves the common **quicksilver** <note>original: *Queck-Silber*</note>. Save the washed-away blackness, and when it has settled, pour off the water. However, melt the silver again with half its weight of **regulus**, amalgamate it with this very same **mercury**, and distill just as before, adding the blackness to the first portion as well. Melt the silver again with half its weight of **regulus**, amalgamate it with the distilled **mercury** as before, and repeat this work 7 or 10 times; thus the **common mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius Vulgi*</note> is prepared, and is henceforth called **Philosophical Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius Philosophicus*; mercury that has been "animated" or purified through successive distillations with metals until it is deemed ready for the Great Work of alchemy</note>. Nevertheless, it must still be distilled 3 or 4 more times by itself out of small, clean retorts.

The **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius*; quicksilver</note> for this work, as it is taken in the beginning, can be merely common, yet pure; but it is better if one, before the **animation** <note>original: *Animation*; the alchemical process of "awakening" or preparing mercury so it can dissolve other metals</note>, makes it into **cinnabar** <note>original: *Zinober*; a bright red mineral consisting of mercury sulfide</note>, revivifies it from the **regulus** <note>original: *Regulo*; the metallic heart of a mineral, usually referring here to the Regulus of Antimony</note>, and only then animates it, thus: Take 1 pound of whole cinnabar (for if one buys it ground, it is falsified with **minium** <note>original: *Minio*; red lead, a common cheap additive</note>) and 1/2 pound of **regulus**. Mix both as well as possible into a very fine powder; put that into a **retort** <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling</note>, attach a **receiver** <note>original: *Vorlage*; a vessel to catch the distilled liquid</note>, and distill. In this way, the **running mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius currens*; liquid metallic mercury</note> passes over, but the common sulfur of the cinnabar will become a new **antimony** <note>original: *Antimonio*</note> with the regulus. The mercury obtained from this work should be made into cinnabar again with pure common sulfur, and revivified anew from the regulus; and one repeats this work several times, thus obtaining an excellently beautiful mercury, which becomes perfected all the sooner through the subsequent animation.

The **antimony** <note>original: *Antimonium*</note> that is formed anew from the regulus and the **sulfur of cinnabar** <note>original: *Sulphure Cinnabaris*</note> likewise yields a **regulus** <note>original: *Regulum*</note> again if one melts it with **iron** <note>original: *Marte*; literally 'Mars', the alchemical name for iron</note>. Of **philosophical gold** <note>original: *Auro Philosophico*; gold that has been processed to be "living" and capable of transformation</note>, how such may be [obtained] **by a shorter way** <note>original: *viâ compendiosâ*</note>

...is still to be briefly reported, concerning what is to be obtained from the prepared **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurio*; quicksilver</note>:

The Animated Mercury now separates this so-called **Sulfur of Venus** from the **Mercurial Body** <note>original: *Corpore Mercuriali*</note> through mere digestion. ...wonderful **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*; quicksilver</note>. For it is always green **on the surface** <note>original: *in superficie*</note>, as the practitioner will see through experience.

For then it would become common gold, because its outward-turned and open power would be turned back inside and locked away. Instead, one should only glow it gently under a clean **muffle** <note>a clay chamber in a furnace that protects the material from direct flame</note>, and it will become a high gold-powder, exceedingly delicate, which quickly mixes with the **Animated Mercury**, and **most benignly** <note>original: *benignissima*</note>...

...united through the smallest parts by **digestion** <note>original: *Digestione*; a process of gentle, continuous heat</note> and opened **radically** <note>original: *radicaliter*; meaning from the root or essence</note>, so that consequently, with easy labor, the **Stone** <note>original: *Lapis*; the Philosophers' Stone</note>, the great **Tincture**, can be manufactured from it.

### **Second Section: Concerning Gold** <note>original: *Sectio secunda de Auro*</note>

In the preceding first **Section** or division, **gold** was treated—specifically how one should put it to use through the **dry way** <note>original: *via sicca*; chemical processes using high heat or fire without liquid solvents</note>, both **particularly** <note>original: *particulariter*; referring to the transmutation of specific metals</note> and **universally** <note>original: *universaliter*; referring to the creation of the universal medicine or Stone</note>. An intelligent **laboratory worker** <note>original: *Laborant*</note> will recognize this with gratitude and will easily judge that no fables or "fishing in the air" <note>original: *Lufft-Fischereyen*; an idiom for idle fantasies or fruitless pursuits</note>, but rather true and rational things, have been prescribed and shared with him. These things, through clever but not difficult labor, can and may easily be brought to a desired and pleasing end with divine help.

Here it shall be taught how gold is also to be transformed and exalted through the **wet way** <note>original: *via humida*; chemical processes involving liquid solvents or acids</note>. This is indeed an undertaking that many have contradicted and will continue to contradict; however, I hold that such contradiction stems from a certain haste. It is certainly true that the ancients knew nothing of wet **corrosive solvents** <note>original: *Corrosivischen Menstruis*; 'menstruum' was the alchemical term for a solvent capable of dissolving solids</note>...

<meta>The catchword "wust" at the bottom of the page likely completes the German word "gewusst" (known), finishing the sentence "the ancients knew nothing of..."</meta>

...[confu]sed, and because their successors followed them in the **dry way** <term>via sicca: chemical processes using high heat or fire rather than liquid solvents</term>, and considered running **mercury** <note>original: *Queck-Silber*</note> to be the only metallic **solvent** <term>Menstruum: a liquid used to dissolve or extract substances</term> and dissolving water, they believed in nothing else. Instead, they even contradicted [other methods], especially when they perhaps saw that one or another who worked in something other than **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurio*</note> did not reach his goal. However, such a thing does not negate the possibility of the matter; nor does it follow that one must know everything, and that which is unknown to him must not be true. Rather, it is more appropriately said: "Alas, how much there is that we do not know!" <note>original: *Heu quantum est, quod nescimus!*</note> 

Therefore, no one should allow themselves to be deterred from attempting metallic works in the **wet way** <note>original: *nassen Wege*; the process of using liquid acids or solvents</note>. One should, however, proceed rationally in this and direct their aim toward nothing else than the refinement and exaltation of **metallic bodies** <note>original: *metallische Cörper*</note>. Consider this: although the corrosive **solvents** <note>original: *Menstrua*</note> are not of a metallic nature, and also have no essential entry into the central mixture of the metals, they can nevertheless pass as instruments or tools and "middle-things" for metallic works. ### Refinement of Gold through the Regulus of Antimony and Sublimated Mercury.

**Take** <term>recipe</term> 1 part **Gold** <note>symbol: ☉</note> that is entirely fine; melt it with 3 parts of the **purest metallic antimony** <term>regulus</term> <note>original: *Reguli purissimi ex ♁ & ♂te parati*</note> prepared from **antimony** <note>symbol: ♁</note> and **iron** <note>symbol: ♂</note>. Crush the mixture into a coarse powder, mix **sublimated mercury** into it, by 3 parts—the mixing should also only be done **roughly** <note>Latin: *grosso modo*</note>—place it in a glass retort and distill first gently, thereafter with a stronger fire, until all the sublimate and metallic antimony have separated from the gold. With a stronger fire, the gold partly rises up as **flowers** <term>a fine powder or crystalline substance formed by sublimation</term> <note>original: *Flores*</note>; one part clings a bit lower on the walls, and a very little remains lying at the bottom of the retort. But put it all together, for it is a broken-down, refined gold.

Now take such a delicate **gold-calx** <term>a fine powder produced by the chemical breakdown of a metal</term> <note>original: *Gold-Kalck*</note>, pour the following **solvent** <term>a liquid used to dissolve a solid substance</term> <note>original: *Menstruum*</note> upon it, place it in **digestion** <term>the process of heating a substance gently over a long period to encourage a reaction</term> and let as much dissolve gently as can. When it no longer dissolves, pour off the dissolved [liquid] into a clean **flask** <note>original: *Kolben-Glaß*</note>, and [pour] fresh **solvent** upon [the remainder],

Therefore, a small supply of solvent is necessary. # This would be a particular process in the wet path.

But one should reflect upon whether a high **tincture** <term>a potent alchemical substance capable of transforming base metals</term> <note>original: *Tinctur*</note> might not be made from it, if one were to bring this **fixed powder** <note>original: *Fixe Pulver*; a substance that has been made resistant to fire</note>—which is in truth an **opened gold** <term>gold that has been chemically broken down to release its internal "virtue" or power</term> whose power already stands outward, and therefore **fixes** <note>original: *figiret*; turns a liquid or volatile substance into a solid that can withstand heat</note> the **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*</note> in such a short time—to a higher state through repeated work. One should do it, for example, thus: **Take** <note>represented by the symbol ℞</note> 1 part of the powder that was prepared from the volatile gold and **animated mercury** <note>original: *☿io Animato*</note>; **dissolve** <note>original: *solvire*</note> it in its own **solvent** <term>a liquid used to dissolve solids, often called a "menstruum"</term> <note>original: *Menstruo*</note>, **volatilize** <note>original: *volatilisire*; to convert into vapor through heat</note> it, as was done with the **flowers of raw gold** <term>the fine powder or sublimate produced by heating gold</term> <note>Latin: *Floribus Auri crudi*</note>, combine this volatilized gold with half the weight of the gold prepared with the mercury, and **coagulate** <note>original: *coagulire*; to thicken or solidify a liquid</note> it. Thus, it will necessarily **tincture** and **fix** (or, in a word, **ripen** <note>original: *zeitigen*; the alchemical process of accelerating the natural growth of metals</note>) the mercury in a shorter time and in greater quantity. Let an experienced person reflect on this; he cannot regret it.

# Here Follows the Solvent or Gold-
# Dissolving Water.

**Take:** <note>represented by the symbol ℞</note> **Sal ammoniac** <note>original: *Salmiac*; ammonium chloride</note>, **saltpeter** <note>original: *Salpeter*; potassium nitrate</note>, **calcined alum** <term>alum that has been heated to a powder to remove its water content</term> <note>original: *calcinirten Alaun*</note>, equal parts of each, 5 pounds, totaling 15 pounds. Grind each until completely clear and mix them all well together. Furthermore, take 15 pounds of **calcined flints** <note>original: *calcinirte Feuer-Steine*; flint stones heated and crushed to a powder</note>, crush them into a fine flour, and mix these as well as possible among the first **ingredients** <note>original: *Species*</note>. Distill these from a **tubulated retort** <term>a glass distilling flask with a side-neck and an additional opening (tubule) on top for adding materials</term> <note>original: *Retorta tubulata*</note>, at whose neck a wide **receiver** <term>a vessel designed to collect the distilled liquid</term> <note>original: *Recipiente*</note> is placed; do it in this manner: When the retort is well-bricked into the stone furnace, place a large receiver before it and seal it with paper. Let the **sealant** <term>a paste, often of clay or lime, used to make joints airtight</term> <note>original: *Lutum*</note> dry, both at the neck of the retort and at the furnace; light the fire gently, otherwise the retort will shatter. Afterward, introduce 4 **Loth** <note>a historical unit of weight, approximately 2 ounces or 60 grams</note> of the materials at a time through the **tube** <note>original: *Tubulum*; the opening on top of the retort</note> with a spoon, and quickly close the tube with its stopper.

->Note.<-

The ingredients must also be thoroughly dried beforehand and introduced while warm; otherwise, everything will burst into a thousand pieces. As soon as the ingredients are introduced and the tube is stoppered...

### Mars or Iron.

Some may wonder that in this treatise, immediately after Gold, Iron is dealt with; but it should be so. For in the Philosophical Heaven, Mars is closest to the Sun, of which the great English Philosopher Anonymous Philaletha <note>Philaletha refers to Eirenaeus Philalethes, a famous 17th-century alchemist; this quote likely refers to his work "An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King"</note> writes thus: From the Northern region <note>original: *Mitternächtigen Gegend*</note> toward the West <note>original: *Abend*</note> a mountain is found, which is one of those seven, and from the highest majesty of the others etc. In this mountain the spirit is hidden that completes our mastery, which is to be understood thus: The seven mountains are the seven metals <term>the seven traditional metals of alchemy: Lead, Tin, Iron, Gold, Copper, Mercury, and Silver</term> <note>original: *Metalla*</note> Lead, Tin, Mars, the Sun, Venus, Mercury, and the Moon. The Northern region toward the West is Lead <note>represented by the symbol for Saturn</note>. The West itself is the Moon. If one now counts from Lead to the Moon, and begins to count from Saturn to the Sun, Mars is closest to the Sun when calculated from this side; it is the second from the highest majesty, that is <note>original: *i. e.* / *id est*</note>, from Saturn; for Saturn is the highest among the planets, after him Jupiter is the first, and

<meta>catchword: Mars</meta>

It describes a symbolic geography where metals are arranged like planets or mountains, placing Mars in close proximity to the Sun (Gold).</summary>

And in another place, when he speaks of the Mercury of the Sages <note>The "philosophical mercury," a refined substance believed to be the root of all metals, not common quicksilver</note>, he says by way of a parable that such is hidden in Mars: "Understand that Mercury, which the Sun, rising in Mars, spreads through all parts of the World (that is, of Mars)." <note>original Latin: *eum intellige Mercurium, quem Sol in Martio (i. e. Marte) oriens per omnia Mundi (i. e. ♂ii) loca diffundit.*</note> About which there is no time here to philosophize, but we wish likewise to go straight to the work, saying only this little more:

In the coarse iron a most refined gold is hidden, which indeed cannot be brought out in any common way; yet one can place this before the eyes of those who are merely "word-wise" but inexperienced <note>original: *Wort-Klugen*; those who know the terminology but lack practical laboratory success</note>, through the <term>chemical art</term> <note>original: *chymische Kunst*; refers here to the practical application of alchemy</note>, of which mention has been made before.

But here it shall be proven even more clearly: Take 1 part Iron, 2 parts **Antimony** <note>original: ♁ium (Antimonium)</note>, let them flow <note>melt</note> together, as is well known; afterward take equal parts **Tartar** <note>potassium bitartrate</note> and **Saltpeter**, pulverize both, and using a spoon, gradually apply some to the molten mixture—about 4 **Loth** <note>a historical unit of weight, approximately 16 grams or half an ounce</note> of this **flux-powder** for every pound of the mixture, so that it might flow more easily. Knock the **slag** <note>stony waste matter</note> from the **regulus** <note>original: *Könige* (King); the refined metallic button that settles at the bottom of a crucible</note> and crush it into a powder; rub an equal weight of **Salmiac** <note>Ammonium chloride</note> into it, rubbing with the greatest diligence; place it into a coated <note>luted with a protective layer of clay</note> glass flask, set it in a **sand-bath** <note>original: *Sand-Capelle*; a container of sand heated to provide even, high temperatures</note>, and give it a fire strong enough that the slag and Salmiac melt together. 

In this melt, the Salmiac rises above the pulverized slag, which is called <term>sublimation</term> <note>the process of a substance transitioning from a solid to a vapor and back to a solid, often used to purify chemicals</note>. In this process, rub the Salmiac back into the slag as thoroughly as possible, let it melt once more, and when it has melted, let it cool; you will find the Salmiac on top again. Mix them together once more, add a little new Salmiac, and repeat this work several times until the slag and Salmiac have mixed well. Then <term>cement</term> <note>a dry process of heating a metal in contact with a powder to alter its properties or "color" it</note> silver with it, and see whether a profitabl- <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "ches" suggests the word "austrägliches," meaning profitable or advantageous.</note>

...which has become its gold from **Mars** <note>original: *Marte*; the alchemical name for iron</note> and has entered into **Luna** <note>original: *Lunam*; the alchemical name for silver</note>, which can easily be extracted through the process of **separation** <note>original: *Scheidung*; the chemical parting of metals</note>. Furthermore, **Mars** [iron] can indeed do nothing in and of itself; but if one takes from it that which it possesses, namely its gold, one can then proceed with it according to the instructions given in the works on gold. If one adds a portion of **copper** to the iron during the operation, it yields much better results. For example:

Knock this button away from the **slag** <note>original: *Schlacken*; the stony waste matter</note>.

For this reason, it must be saved until it is needed.

Here, however, it shall be demonstrated even more clearly: Take 1 part iron and 2 parts <term>Antimony</term> <note>original: $\delta$ium (Antimonium)</note>; let them melt [flow], as is well known. Afterward, take equal amounts of **tartar** and **saltpeter**, pulverize both, and gradually add some of this to the molten mixture with a spoon—about 4 **Loth** <note>a historical unit of weight, approximately 16 grams or half an ounce</note> of this flux-powder for every pound of the mixture—so that it may melt all the more easily. 

Strike the **slag** <note>original: *Schlacken*; the stony waste matter produced during the smelting of ore</note> away from the <term>regulus</term> <note>original: *Könige* (king); the heavy, purified metal button that settles at the bottom of a crucible</note> and crush it into a powder. In this process, grind the sal ammoniac back into the slag as well as possible and let it melt again; and when it has melted, let it cool, and you will find the sal ammoniac on top once more. Mix them together again, add a little new sal ammoniac as well, and repeat this work several times until the slag and the sal ammoniac have mixed thoroughly. The "butter" obtained from this work is coagulated into a calx of Jupiter <note>original: *Calcem Jovis*; tin oxide. In alchemy, Jupiter is the name for Tin</note>. If one refines this through cupellation <note>original: *treibet solchen ab*; a refining process where lead is burnt off to leave precious metals</note>, one will also obtain Silver and Gold.

# Another More Significant Work
### From Iron <note>original: *ex Marte*; in alchemy, Mars is the symbol and name for Iron.</note>.

Dissolve Iron in *Aqua fortis* <note>original: *Aqua fort*; nitric acid, literally "strong water"</note> and distill all the *Aqua fortis* away from it again, finally using a very strong fire. In this way, you will obtain a subtle *Crocum* <note>original: *Crocum*; literally "saffron," referring here to a fine reddish-brown iron oxide powder</note>. Reverberate <note>to heat in a furnace where the heat is reflected back onto the material</note> this powder in a closed vessel <note>original: *in clauso vase*</note> under a muffle <note>an oven-like chamber used to isolate the material from direct flame</note> for three weeks; thus the iron will be well calcined <note>reduced to a stable powder through heat</note>. Mix this with sal ammoniac <note>original: *Salmiac*; ammonium chloride</note> and sublimate it. The mercurial part <note>the volatile portion of the metal</note> will rise up with the sal ammoniac; preserve this carefully.

Calcine the residue <note>original: *Remanenz*</note> again for 8 days under the muffle, and afterwards extract the salt with distilled vinegar <note>original: *Aceto destillato*</note>. Pour off the extracted liquid and let the vinegar evaporate gently until a skin forms on the surface. Place it in a cellar, and yellow crystals will form. 

Dissolve these crystals in fresh vinegar and add to them that which was sublimated with the sal ammoniac. Digest <note>to subject to steady, gentle heat</note> this gently for 30 days. Afterward, distill and cohobate <note>the process of repeatedly pouring a distilled liquid back onto its residue and re-distilling it to concentrate its "virtue"</note> it as many times as necessary until it has all come over as a single yellow liquor.

Take 1 part Gold and dissolve it in rectified *Aqua Regia* <note>original: *Aqua Regis*; "Royal Water," a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving gold</note>. Also dissolve 12 parts of common mercury <note>original: *Mercurii vulg.*</note> in *Aqua Regia*. Pour these solutions together and draw off <note>original: *abstrahire*; to remove or evaporate</note> all the *Aqua Regia* very gently,

### Yet Another Work from Iron <note>original: *ex Marte*</note>.

Dissolve a <term>very subtle Crocus of Iron <note>original: *Crocum subtilissimum Martis*; a fine reddish powder made from calcined iron</note></term> in the <term>Circulat <note>a highly refined solvent, typically a "circulated" or purified spirit</note></term>, digest the solution for one month, thereafter cohobate <note>to distill a liquid and pour it back over the solid residue repeatedly</note> until everything passes over; then the <term>mercurial part of the metal <note>original: *pars Mercurialis Metalli*</note></term> falls to the bottom, but on top remains the <term>Sulfur of Iron <note>original: *Sulphur Martis*; the essential "combustible" principle or oil extracted from iron</note></term> in the form of a yellow, thick <term>liquor</term>, which is very corrosive. This is separated through a small glass funnel and sweetened <note>original: *edulcoriret*; washed or neutralized</note> with <term>Spirit of Wine <note>original: *Spiritu Vini*; high-proof alcohol or ethanol</note></term>; thus one has an excellent medicine.

However, one proceeds best with it in this way: Take a red <term>precipitated Mercury <note>original: *Mercurium præcipitatum*; likely red mercuric oxide</note></term>, and pour the <term>Sulfur of Iron <note>original: *Sulphur Martis*</note></term> onto it while it is still mixed in the <term>Circulat</term>, as much as the <term>precipitate</term> can swallow up and accept, and no more; afterward pour <term>most highly rectified Spirit of Wine <note>original: *Spiritum Vini rectificatissimum*</note></term>

**Spirit of Wine** away from it again, yet not
entirely to dryness; repeat such
pouring-on and drawing-off of the **Spirit of Wine**,
until it has carried over all the **corrosive**;
which is known to be finished when the **Spirit**
**of Wine** comes over again in its own taste and
smell. In such a manner
one obtains an excellent **precipitate** <note>original: *Præcipitat*</note>,
which is a good medicine, [and] also can be melted
into true gold, of
which more [will be said] in the following section **concerning Copper** <note>original: *de Venere*; Venus is the alchemical name for copper</note>.

## Concerning Venus, or Copper.

Just as Mars <note>Iron</note> is closest to the Sun from above, so Venus <note>Copper</note> is closest from below, and therefore follows Mars here in the sequence; and because in very many processes Venus and Mars are together, and neither wishes to be without the other—as has already been seen from what preceded—this chapter **concerning Venus** <note>original: *de Venere*</note> will also frequently mention Mars. Venus, however, consists of a white **Mercury** <term>Mercurio; the fluidic, metallic principle of matter</term> and a green earth or **Sulfur** <term>Sulphur; the combustible or coloring principle</term>; and all those who seek or claim a red **Sulfur** in copper <note>represented by the symbol ♀</note> are in error.

To clearly prove this, the following process is sufficient. Dissolve copper in the **Circulat** <note>a high-grade alchemical solvent</note>, place it for 4 weeks in gentle **digestion** <note>slow, consistent heating</note>, thereafter **distill** half of it away, pour fresh **Circulat** upon it, **digest** it again, and **distill** it; do this so often until all the copper has passed over <note>has been carried over through the distillation process</note>. Then **digest** it for another 4 weeks, and thus the **Sulfur** separates from its **Mercury** in this manner: the **mercurial part** <note>original: *Pars Mercurialis*</note> remains in the **solvent** <note>original: *Menstruo*</note>, while the **Sulfur** settles to the bottom as a green **liquid** <note>original: *Liquor*</note>; separate these two. **Sweeten** <term>edulcorire; to wash away salts or acids until the substance is tasteless</term> the **Sulfur** with **Spirit**...

To **proceed in particular** <term>Particulariter; refers to a "particular" alchemical work aimed at transmuting a specific metal, rather than the "universal" work of the Philosopher's Stone</term> with **Venus** <note>copper</note> so that one may derive benefit from it, dissolve copper in common **parting-water** <term>Scheid-Waſſer; nitric acid, used to "part" or separate metals like gold and silver</term>. The metal begins to flow with the sal ammoniac like pitch. Increase the fire as much as you can, and a portion of ♀ <note>the symbol for Venus or Copper</note> **sublimes** <note>turns directly from a solid to a vapor and collects as a solid again in the neck of the flask</note>. However, most of it remains behind as a liquid **mass** <note>original: *Massa*</note>, which turns green in the air.

This **mass**, when it is melted and **reduced** <note>returned to a metallic state</note> with silver, always yields gold. One has gold and silver

Reduce this slag with lead by boiling and **cupellation** <note>original: "abtreiben"; a refining process where ores or alloys are heated under high temperatures to separate precious metals from base metals like lead</note>. In this way, you will recover the silver, and it will be very rich in gold besides. 

**Note Well:** The slag must first be crushed small and boiled with a strong **lye** made of **quicklime** <note>calcium oxide</note> and **potash**, so that nothing is lost. The gold **regulus** is then melted with this silver, adding as much additional silver as is necessary for **inquartation** <term>Quartirung; a method of separating gold from silver by adding silver until the gold is only one-quarter of the alloy, allowing acid to dissolve the silver more effectively</term>, and then perform the inquartation...

When vitriols are combined through frequent dissolving <note>original: solviren</note>, coagulating <note>original: coaguliren</note>, and distilling, one obtains a sweet, so-called oil; however, it requires great labor and a long time. ## On Antimony or Stibnite.

**Antimony** or <term>stibnite</term> <note>original: Spieß-Glaß; the common mineral form of antimony sulfide</note> is a subject upon which a beginning <term>chemist</term> <note>original: Chymicus</note>, who has a desire to learn, can practice. Its <term>immediate constituents</term> <note>original: Constitutiva proxima; the fundamental components of a substance</note> are (1) a running quicksilver, (2) burning sulfur, and (3) white arsenical earth, as can be <term>plainly demonstrated</term> <note>original: ad oculum; literally "to the eye"</note> by the chemical art, and shall be brought to light in this treatise; we will therefore divide this chapter into two sections. In the first, we will deal with the decomposition of this mineral into its <term>primary constituents</term> <note>original: prima constitutiva</note>; in the second, however, we will teach how one should prepare all kinds of metallic and medicinal tinctures, and finally the <term>Stone of Fire</term> <note>original: Lapidem Ignis; a legendary alchemical substance believed to be a potent medicine or transmutative agent</note>.

## Section One.

To demonstrate the **primary constituents** <note>original: Constitutiva prima; the fundamental building blocks of a substance</note> of **Antimony**, we shall first cite only common laboratory procedures.

**Take** <note>original: R., the symbol for "Recipe"</note> 1 pound of **Antimony** <note>indicated by the symbol ♁</note>; let it flow in a sturdy melting crucible, and add to it half a pound of **potash salt** <note>original: Pott-Aschen-Salz; potassium carbonate</note>. Melt it strongly, and the **alkaline salt** <term>Alcalische Salz</term> <note>a basic substance used to neutralize or dissolve specific components</note> of the potash will dissolve the burning sulfur of the antimony, turning into what is called **slag** <note>original: Schlacken; the stony waste matter separated from metals during smelting</note>. The **mercurial part** <term>mercurialische Theil</term> <note>the metallic essence</note>, however, along with the **white arsenical earth** <term>Arsenicalischen Erde</term>, being the heavier metallic portion, falls to the bottom. This is called the **regulus of antimony** <term>Regulum Antimonii</term> <note>the purified metallic mass of the antimony</note>. 

Crush the slag into small pieces, put them in a glazed pot, pour water over them, and boil it. The water will turn red; pour off the water while it is still warm and splash a little vinegar or **spirit of salt** <term>Spiritum Salis</term> <note>hydrochloric acid</note> into it. Thereupon the **sulfur** <term>Schwefel</term> falls to the bottom as a light red powder. When this powder is dried and placed in a glowing crucible, it flames a little—though not properly, due to the adhering alkaline salt—but it possesses a perfect smell of sulfur, just like common sulfur mixed with potash

The rest, however, clings firmly to the sides of the flask’s neck and is full of the metallic regulus; add this to the "flowers."

As for what remained **at the bottom** <note>original Latin: in fundo</note>, mix it with new sal ammoniac and sublimate it again. A loose dust will remain behind which glows like a coal when put into a red-hot crucible; this is a part of the **arsenical essence** <term>Arsenicalischen Wesens</term> <note>the volatile and often toxic mineral component</note>. Now, rub everything that has sublimated together as finely as possible and add two parts of **potash salt** <term>Pott-Aschen-Salz</term> <note>potassium carbonate</note>, calculated in proportion to the sal ammoniac. Finally, let all moisture evaporate, and when it is well dried, rub it until quite clear with **spirit of salt** <term>Spiritu Salis</term> <note>hydrochloric acid</note>, adding only a little spirit of salt; afterwards place it into a **retort** <term>Retorte</term> <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-sloping neck used for distillation</note> with a wide neck. ### First Tincture.
<note>original: *Tinctura prima*</note>

Take the slag that is made with *potash* <term>potash</term> <note>original: *Pott-Asche*; an alkaline salt often used as a flux in melting minerals</note>, as mentioned on page 65. Pour off the *tincture*, and add fresh *spirit* to the residue until all the *tincture* is *extracted* <note>original: *extrahiret*</note>. Pour the *extractions* <note>original: *Extractiones*</note> together and distill the *spirit* until half of it has evaporated; thus you will obtain a beautiful, thick, dark-red *tincture*. This is an excellent *blood cleanser* <term>blood cleanser</term> <note>original: *Blut-Reinigung*</note>, to be taken in doses of 5 to 15 drops at a time, in wine or whatever liquid one prefers.
